=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a simple undirected graph with N vertices and M edges, with vertices labeled 1 to N and edges labeled 1 to M. The i-th edge connects vertices U_i and V_i. Initially, G does not contain an odd cycle.
Takahashi and Aoki will play a game using this graph G. With Aoki going first, they take turns performing the following operation:

- Choose a pair of integers (i,j) with 1 \leq i < j \leq N that satisfies both of the following conditions, then add an edge connecting vertices i and j to G.
- G does not already have an edge connecting vertices i and j.
- Adding an edge connecting vertices i and j does not create an odd cycle.



A player who cannot perform this operation loses, and the other player wins.
Determine who wins when both players play optimally.

What is an odd cycle?
A sequence of vertices (v_0,v_1,\ldots,v_k) of G is called an odd cycle if and only if all of the following conditions are satisfied:

- k is odd.
- v_0=v_k.
- For every 1\leq i \leq k, there is an edge connecting v_{i-1} and v_{i}.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N M
U_1 V_1
U_2 V_2
\vdots
U_M V_M

Output

If Aoki (the first player) wins, print Aoki; otherwise, if Takahashi (the second player) wins, print Takahashi.

Constraints


- 1 \leq N \leq 2\times 10^5
- 0 \leq M \leq 2\times 10^5
- 1 \leq U_i < V_i \leq N
- The given graph does not contain an odd cycle.
- The given graph does not contain multi-edges.
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

4 3
1 2
2 3
3 4

Sample Output 1

Aoki

If Aoki (the first player) adds the edge (1,4), Takahashi (the second player) cannot move. Thus, Aoki wins.

Sample Input 2

4 2
1 2
3 4

Sample Output 2

Takahashi

No matter how Aoki plays, Takahashi wins.

Sample Input 3

9 5
2 9
2 3
4 6
5 7
1 8

Sample Output 3

Aoki

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's formalize state as a collection of connected bipartite components. Each component has:
- color class sizes (a,b)
- number of missing internal edges m = a*b - e (where e is current edge count within component).
Internal moves: choose a missing cross edge, m -> m-1. This is like a heap of m tokens; a move removes one token. No other effect.
Connecting moves: choose two components with sizes (a1,b1,m1), (a2,b2,m2), and choose orientation/merge. New component has color sizes either (a1+a2, b1+b2) with e = e1+e2+1, or (a1+b2, b1+a2) with e = e1+e2+1. New missing m' = A*B - (e1+e2+1). We can express m' in terms of m1,m2 and sizes. Let's compute.

For orientation same (A=a1+a2, B=b1+b2):
m' = (a1+a2)(b1+b2) - e1 - e2 -1
= a1b1 + a1b2 + a2b1 + a2b2 - e1 - e2 -1
= (a1b1 - e1) + (a2b2 - e2) + a1b2 + a2b1 -1
= m1 + m2 + a1 b2 + a2 b1 -1.
But note a1 b2 + a2 b1 is number of legal cross edges between components under orientation? Yes cross-color pairs count. We add one of them, so remaining cross-color pairs become internal missing edges. So m' = m1 + m2 + (cross_edges_opposite - 1). Total legal moves after merge = m' = previous missing internal + cross_edges_opposite -1. Before merge, legal moves included m1+m2 internal plus all n1*n2 cross edges (because can choose orientation). But after merge, cross edges between components are reduced to internal missing cross-color edges under chosen orientation; edges within same color are illegal forever (would create odd cycle). So connecting move consumes one legal move but also destroys some potential cross moves (those same-color under chosen orientation) by making them illegal? Wait adding edge doesn't destroy existing edges; but after merging, edges between same color classes are not legal. Before merging, they were legal (by choosing opposite orientation). So choosing a cross edge with a particular orientation determines that the other same-color pairs become illegal. Thus a move can reduce the total number of future legal moves by more than 1. This is key.

For orientation flip (A=a1+b2, B=b1+a2):
m' = (a1+b2)(b1+a2) - e1 - e2 -1
= a1b1 + a1a2 + b2b1 + b2a2 - e1 - e2 -1
= m1 + m2 + a1 a2 + b1 b2 -1.
Here cross_edges_opposite under flipped orientation count = a1*a2 + b1*b2. So same formula with chosen orientation's cross-color pairs.

Actually a player can always choose a connecting move if at least two components, regardless of m. Filling moves are optional. So players can decide the order: they can connect components immediately, leaving fillings for later, or fill first. Since filling moves don't affect component structure, they are like "passes" that consume a turn. In normal play, having extra pass moves can flip who makes the next important move. This is exactly the theory of "waiting moves": a heap of m tokens where each move removes one token is equivalent to a Nim heap of size m mod2; but because players can choose to take a token or not, it can be used to adjust parity of the number of moves before a forced event. Types: OO=(1,1), OE=(1,0), EO=(0,1), EE=(0,0).

For pair (X,Y), H2 = 1 if at least one of c1,c2 odd.

We can compute:
- OO with OO: c1=c2=n2 mod2 =0 (since n2 even) -> H=0.
- OO with OE: n2=1 odd -> c1=c2=1 -> H=1.
- OO with EO: H=1.
- OO with EE: n2=2 even -> c=0 -> H=0.
- OE with OE: type1 (1,0), type2 (1,0): c1=1, c2=0 -> H=1.
- OE with EO: (1,0),(0,1): c1=0, c2=1 -> H=1.
- OE with EE: (1,0),(0,0): c1=0,c2=0 -> H=0.
- EO with EO: (0,1),(0,1): c1=0,c2=1 -> H=1.
- EO with EE: H=0.
- EE with EE: H=0.

So H2=0 for pairs: OO-OO, OO-EE, OE-EE, EO-EE, EE-EE. H2=1 otherwise.

Can we find f(type) such that f(X) xor f(Y) = H2? Let's assign variables:
f(OO)=x, f(OE)=y, f(EO)=z, f(EE)=w.
Equations:
x xor x =0 = H2(OO,OO)=0 OK.
x xor w = H2(OO,EE)=0 => x=w.
y xor w = H2(OE,EE)=0 => y=w.
z xor w =0 => z=w.
w xor w=0 OK.
So x=y=z=w. Then all f equal, but H2(OE,OE)=1 would require y xor y=0, contradiction. So H2 is not XOR of individual f. Thus SG not simply XOR of component values if F is XOR.

Maybe we can solve directly using strategy stealing/pairing.

Let's think about the game in terms of final bipartition. Each move adds an edge between two vertices that are currently in opposite parts. Once an edge is added, it cannot be removed. The final graph is a complete bipartite graph K_{A,B}. The set of possible final graphs corresponds to all proper 2-colorings of the initial graph (with isolated vertices colored arbitrarily). The game is: players alternately choose edges from the complete bipartite graph corresponding to a coloring that is consistent with previous choices, until all such edges are chosen. This is exactly the "achievement game" on the Boolean lattice of colorings? Maybe can be seen as playing on a graph where each vertex has a color bit; initially each connected component has a fixed bit up to flip; isolated vertices free. A move chooses two vertices and sets their relative color to opposite (if not already determined), and adds the edge. If their relative color already determined opposite, it's a "filler" move. If determined same, illegal. If undetermined (different components), it determines relative color.

Thus the game has two kinds of decisions:
- Determining relative colors between components (connecting moves).
- Filling edges between vertices whose relative color is already determined opposite.

Once all relative colors determined (graph connected), only filling moves remain.

This is analogous to a game on a graph of "variables" (components) where each variable has a domain of two colors and a weight (number of vertices in each color). A connecting move between two variables chooses a relation (same/opposite) and merges them; the number of filler moves created depends on the relation and weights. Filler moves are just tokens.

Could be solved by considering the "parity" of the total number of moves under optimal play; maybe because filler moves are "dominated" by their parity, and the merge game is a "normal play" on a complete graph with vertex weights. Let's try to derive a recursive outcome formula using the concept of "cold" games? Since all options are just adding edges, maybe the game is "weakly acyclic" and has a simple "strategy stealing" by pairing edges.

So for N even, outcome may be determined by whether the first player can force final A parity to target. This is a game of choosing orientations. Maybe it reduces to a simple parity game on the graph of components: Each component has a "weight" parity w_i = a_i mod2? Wait contribution of component to A parity depends on orientation: if not flipped, contribution a_i mod2; if flipped, contribution b_i mod2 = (n_i - a_i) mod2 = n_i mod2 XOR a_i mod2. Since n_i parity matters. For N even, total N even, but component sizes can be odd/even.

Let's define for each component i:
- s_i = a_i mod2 (0 or1)
- t_i = b_i mod2 = (n_i mod2) XOR s_i.
Flipping toggles contribution if s_i != t_i, i.e., if n_i is odd. If n_i even, s_i = t_i, so flipping doesn't change contribution parity. Thus components of even order have fixed contribution parity (equal to a_i mod2, which is same as b_i mod2). Components of odd order have a "free bit" that can be toggled by orientation.

Also isolated vertices are odd-order components with s=1,t=0 (free bit). A component with odd number of vertices always has one color class odd, one even, so its contribution parity can be either 0 or1 depending on orientation. Thus only odd-order components contribute flexibility. Even-order components have fixed parity contribution.

Therefore the final A parity is:
P_final = (sum over even-order components of a_i mod2) XOR (sum over odd-order components of chosen bit x_i) mod2, where each odd-order component can choose x_i ∈ {0,1} (which side gets the odd color class? Actually contribution parity = a_i mod2 if not flipped, b_i mod2 = a_i mod2 XOR1 if flipped, so x_i is a free bit). So the set of possible final A parities is: base B0 = parity of even components' a_i, plus the XOR of k_odd free bits. The possible sums of k free bits are all parities if k_odd >0; if k_odd=0, fixed B0. More precisely, if there is at least one odd-order component, both parities are achievable (by flipping that component). If no odd-order components, final A parity is fixed.

Let's analyze the game in terms of final A parity. For N even, target r = 1 XOR M mod2. The final A parity is determined by orientations. The game of adding edges can be seen as players choosing edges; but maybe the final A parity is actually determined by the parity of the number of moves made before the graph becomes connected? Let's derive.

When a connecting move merges two components, it fixes their relative orientation. The final A parity is the XOR of chosen orientation bits for odd-order components. The number of odd-order components changes when merging:
- Merging two odd-order components: result even-order (odd+odd=even). This consumes two free bits and fixes their XOR? Actually the relative orientation between two odd components determines the parity of their combined contribution. If both are odd-order, each has a free bit; after merging, the combined component has even order, so its contribution parity is fixed (the XOR of the two chosen bits). Thus one bit of freedom is eliminated (the relative orientation), and the other becomes fixed? More concretely, two odd components with sizes (a,b) and (c,d) where a,b opposite parity, c,d opposite. After choosing orientation, the merged component has even order, so its contribution parity is fixed (either 0 or1). The final A parity will include this fixed value. The players' choice of orientation determines that fixed value.
- Merging an odd-order component with an even-order component: result odd-order. The even component has fixed contribution; the odd component's free bit may be toggled? Actually merging an odd free component with an even fixed component: the resulting odd component still has one free bit (the orientation of the whole merged component relative to outside), but its internal relative orientation is fixed. The free bit count remains 1? Let's see: odd + even = odd. There is one free bit overall (flip the merged component), so the odd component's free bit persists; the even component's fixed contribution is absorbed. The choice of orientation may set the internal relation, affecting the base parity but not the number of free bits.
- Merging two even-order components: result even-order, no free bits; orientation choice may affect fixed contribution? Even components have fixed contribution parity regardless of flip? Wait an even-order component has a_i and b_i same parity. If both even, contribution parity 0; if both odd, contribution parity 1. Flipping swaps same parity, so contribution parity unchanged. Thus even components have fixed contribution parity independent of orientation. Merging two even components: result even-order, contribution parity = sum of fixed parities, independent of orientation. So orientation choice doesn't affect final A parity! But it affects c parity and total moves? Wait if final A parity fixed, total moves parity fixed; orientation choices that don't change final A parity might still change total moves? No total moves parity = A parity xor M, so if A parity fixed, total parity fixed. But c choices could have different parity? Let's check two even components with both c even? Earlier P pairs for even-even had both c even. So indeed c parity even, no effect. If even components with both color classes odd (OO) and even with even (EE), c even. So orientation choices don't change parity. Good.

Thus the strategic game is about the free bits of odd-order components. Each odd-order component has a free bit (which side gets the odd color class). The final A parity is the XOR of these free bits plus a constant base from even components and internal choices. A connecting move between two odd-order components removes two free bits and replaces them with one fixed bit (determined by the relative orientation choice). This reduces the number of free bits by 2? Wait two odd components have 2 free bits. After merging, result even-order has 0 free bits. So free bits decrease by 2. The fixed bit value is chosen by the player making the move (via orientation). This is like a move that removes two free tokens and sets a bit.
A connecting move between odd and even: free bits decrease by 0? Odd component free bit remains, even fixed absorbed. The move may set a base bit but doesn't change number of free bits.
A connecting move between even and even: free bits unchanged (0).

Therefore the number of odd-order components decreases by 2 when merging two odd components, and unchanged otherwise. Since total N even, the number of odd-order components is even. The game of orientation choices can be seen as: there are K odd-order components (K even). They will eventually be merged among themselves or with even components. To connect all components into one, if there are even components, odd components may be absorbed one by one without reducing K until two odd components merge? Actually to eliminate all free bits, every odd-order component must eventually be merged with another odd-order component (because final component has even order, so odd-order components must pair up). Let's analyze odd-odd merge. Let component1 odd: (a,b) with a,b opposite parity. Component2 odd: (c,d) opposite. The two orientation choices:
- Orientation same (A=a+c, B=b+d): contribution parity A mod2 = a+c mod2.
- Orientation flipped (A=a+d, B=b+c): contribution parity = a+d mod2.
Since c and d opposite, a+c and a+d are opposite. So the two orientations yield opposite contribution parities. Both orientations are legal if c1>0 and c2>0. c1 = a*c + b*d, c2 = a*d + b*c. For two isolated, c1=1,c2=0, so only one legal. Thus for odd-odd merges where at least one component has both color classes nonempty, the player can choose the contribution parity freely. If both are isolated, the contribution parity is fixed to 1? Merging two isolated vertices creates an edge component (1,1) with contribution 1. Yes.

Let's attempt to derive P/N using the concept of "invariant" under p-preserving moves. Since p=1 is N, P-states must have p=0. Let's focus on p=0 and consider only moves that keep p=0 (c odd). Moves that make p=1 go to N, so they are not useful for moving to P. Thus the P/N among p=0 states is determined by the game where players can either:
- Make a c-odd connecting move, staying in p=0.
- Make any other move, which goes to p=1 (N for opponent), so if you are trying to win, you wouldn't do that unless no c-odd moves? Actually if you are in an N-state, you need a move to P. Since p=1 states are N, not P, moves to p=1 are not winning. So winning moves must be c-odd connecting moves to P. If no such move, the state is P (assuming p=0). Therefore P/N in p=0 is exactly the normal-play outcome of the "c-odd merge game" where a move is a connecting move with c odd, and terminal positions (no c-odd moves) are P. Because if no c-odd moves, all moves go to p=1 N, so current is P. If there is a c-odd move to a P-state, current is N. This is a standard impartial game on components where edges are allowed between components with c odd.

So we need analyze the game where a move merges two components with c odd. What are the c-odd pairs?
From parity table:
- Odd-odd: always exactly one orientation with c odd (unless c=0? but odd-odd non-isolated both orientations positive; for two isolated, c odd orientation legal, c even orientation c=0 illegal). So any two odd-order components can be merged with c odd.
- Odd-even: c odd iff the even component is type OO (both color classes odd). If even component is EE, c even.
- Even-even: never c odd.

Thus in the c-odd merge game:
- You can merge two odd components (O decreases by2).
- You can merge an odd component with an OO even component (O unchanged, but the odd component's type may change? Let's see result type).
- You cannot merge EE even components with anything (except? odd-EE c even, even-even c even). So EE components are "dead" in the c-odd game; they don't participate in winning moves to P. But they still exist and can be merged with c even, leading to p=1 N, which is bad for the player trying to move to P. In a P-state, any move including c-even goes to N, so okay. In an N-state, c-even moves are not winning.

Therefore P/N depends only on the c-odd merge game among odd components and OO even components. EE components and p=0 are irrelevant except they contribute to p? p=0 already.

Let's define:
- Odd components: each has a "type" OE or EO (which color class is odd). But since orientation is free, maybe the type can be toggled by merging with an OO component? Let's analyze c-odd merge odd + OO.

Let odd component X with type OE (a odd,b even) or EO (a even,b odd). Let even OO component Y (c odd,d odd). c_same and c_flip are both odd. The merged component is odd-order (odd+even=odd). What is its type?
For X=OE (a odd,b even), Y=OO (c odd,d odd):
- Orientation same: A = a+c = odd+odd = even, B = b+d = even+odd = odd -> type EO.
- Orientation flipped: A = a+d = odd+odd = even, B = b+c = even+odd = odd -> type EO.
So both orientations produce EO. Thus merging OE with OO flips type to EO.
For X=EO, Y=OO:
- Same: A = a+c = even+odd = odd, B = b+d = odd+odd = even -> OE.
- Flip: A = a+d = even+odd = odd, B = b+c = odd+odd = even -> OE.
So EO + OO -> OE.
Thus merging an odd component with an OO even component flips its type (OE↔EO) and consumes the OO component? Wait the OO component is merged into the odd component, so the number of odd components remains 1, the OO component disappears. So O decreases? Odd + even = odd, so O unchanged (one odd replaced by one odd), but the even OO component is gone. Thus the count of OO even components decreases by1, and the odd component's type flips.

Merging two odd components with c odd:
Let X type OE/EO, Y type OE/EO. The result is an even component. What type (OO or EE) does it have?
We computed earlier for odd-odd c odd orientation yields EE? Let's verify with actual sizes.
X=OE (a odd,b even), Y=OE (c odd,d even). c odd orientation is flip: A = a+d = odd+even = odd? Wait a odd + d even = odd, B = b+c = even+odd = odd -> type OO! Earlier I said EE, let's recalc carefully.
For OE+OE:
c_same = a*d + b*c = odd*even + even*odd = even+even = even.
c_flip = a*c + b*d = odd*odd + even*even = odd+even = odd.
c_flip orientation: A = A1 ∪ B2? Need define orientation flip: If original components have color classes (A1,B1) and (A2,B2). Flipping component2 means its A2 becomes B, B2 becomes A. Then new A = A1 ∪ B2, new B = B1 ∪ A2. So A parity = a + d (since B2 size d), B parity = b + c. For OE+OE: a odd,d even => A odd; b even,c odd => B odd. So result OO. Good.
X=OE, Y=EO (c even,d odd). c odd orientation? c_same = a*d + b*c = odd*odd + even*even = odd+even = odd. c_same orientation (no flip): A = a+c = odd+even = odd, B = b+d = even+odd = odd -> OO. So odd-odd c odd always produces an OO even component. The c even orientation produces EE.
Thus merging two odd components with c odd consumes two odd components and creates one OO even component. O decreases by2, and the number of OO even components increases by1.

Merging odd with OO consumes one OO and flips the odd type, O unchanged.

Merging odd with EE or even-even are c-even, not in c-odd game.

So the c-odd merge game has:
- Tokens: odd components (each with a type bit t ∈ {0,1} representing OE/EO), and OO even components.
- Move 1: choose two odd components. Remove them, add one OO even component. (O decreases by2, OO count +1)
- Move 2: choose one odd component and one OO even component. Remove the OO, flip the odd component's type bit. (O unchanged, OO count -1)

EE even components are inert (do not affect c-odd game). Also, what about odd components that are isolated (1,0) type OE; they are just odd tokens.

This is a much simpler impartial game! Let's denote:
- k = number of odd components (O).
- e = number of OO even components (even-order components with both color classes odd).
- The type bits of odd components: but Move 2 flips a bit; Move 1 ignores bits. Does the actual value of type bits matter for future moves? Move 1 can pair any two odd components regardless of type. Move 2 requires an OO and flips an odd's type. Since type only changes by flipping and Move 1 doesn't depend on type, the distribution of type bits is irrelevant! Because Move 2 can be applied to any odd component and flips it; but since Move 1 doesn't care, and there is no other dependence, the type bits are completely irrelevant. Wait is Move 2 legal for any odd component and any OO? Yes c odd for all odd-OO pairs. The result flips the odd type, but type doesn't matter. Thus the game state is just (k, e) where k = number of odd components, e = number of OO even components. EE even components are ignored for c-odd game.

Let's verify with S and T:
S: one edge (1,1) is OO even -> e=1; four isolated odd -> k=4. State (k=4,e=1).
T: path3 (2,1) is odd (type EO) -> k=1? Wait T has path3 odd + three isolated odd => k=4, e=0. Yes T is (4,0).
In c-odd game:
- From (4,1), Move 2 (odd+OO) -> k=4,e=0 (T). So (4,1) is N if (4,0) is P.
- From (4,0), only Move 1 (odd+odd) -> k=2,e=1. Let's see (2,1) is N? If (2,1) N, then (4,0) has move to N only, so P. Good.

Now we need solve the impartial game on (k,e) with moves:
- A: if k>=2: (k,e) -> (k-2, e+1)  (merge two odd components with c odd)
- B: if k>=1 and e>=1: (k,e) -> (k, e-1)  (merge odd with OO; odd type flips but k,e unchanged except e--)

Note: Move B reduces e by1, k unchanged. Move A reduces k by2, increases e by1.

EE even components and m parity p=0 are ignored. But what about initial m parity p? We assumed p=0 for P-states. If p=1, N. So overall P iff p=0 AND the (k,e) game is P.

Let's test this game on examples:
- Sample2: two edges (1,1) are OO even -> k=0,e=2. p=0. (0,2): no moves -> P. Good.
- Sample1: connected path4 (2,2) is EE even -> k=0,e=0, p=1 -> N. Good.
- Empty N=4: four isolated -> k=4,e=0,p=0. (4,0): only move A to (2,1). Need evaluate (2,1).
- Empty N=2: k=2,e=0. (2,0): move A to (0,1). (0,1): no moves -> P. So (2,0) is N. Good.
- Edge+two isolated: one edge OO e=1, two isolated k=2. (2,1): moves: A -> (0,2) P? (0,2) no moves P. So (2,1) is N. Also B -> (2,0) N. Thus N. Good.
- Path+isolated: path3 odd k=1? Wait path3 is odd, isolated odd => k=2, e=0. (2,0) N. Good.

- T: path3+3 isolated: k=4,e=0. Move A -> (2,1) N. So T P.
Let's compute (k,e) game fully.

Moves:
A: (k,e) -> (k-2, e+1) if k>=2.

Terminal: no moves if k<2 and e=0. Note if e>0 and k=0, no moves? B requires k>=1, A requires k>=2. So (0,e) terminal regardless e. Thus OO even components alone are inert in c-odd game (they cannot be merged with c odd). They can be merged with c even in real game leading to p=1 N, but not winning.

   - c even: e unchanged, p toggles. Result EE.

Filling move: p toggles, k,e unchanged.

Let's formalize the state abstraction:
For each connected component, define:
- n = number of vertices.
- a,b = sizes of bipartition.
- m = a*b - (number of edges in component) = number of missing edges inside component (legal internal moves).
Let k = #components with n odd.
Let e = #components with n even and a,b both odd.
Let p = Σ m mod 2.

We claim the position is losing iff p=0 and (k != 2) and (k==0 or e is even).

We need prove by induction. We can use the c-odd game reasoning but need account for all moves. Let's attempt a rigorous proof.

First, observe that if p=1, the position is winning. Is that always true? We need a move to a losing position. We can show:
- If there is a filling move (m>0), it changes p to0 and leaves k,e. If the resulting position is losing, done.

Instead of proving p=1 always winning separately, we can prove the full P condition by induction on the total number of legal moves? But total moves can be large; need show from any state not satisfying P, there exists a move to a state satisfying P, and from any state satisfying P, all moves go to states not satisfying P. This is the standard P-position proof. We can do this by analyzing move effects on (k,e,p) and showing the condition is consistent.

Let's compute the actual P/N for (k,e,p) more generally. We need solve the full game on (k,e,p) with transitions including p toggles. Let's do that.

We can model the game at the abstraction level (k,e,p) with moves:
- Filling: p -> 1-p, k,e unchanged. (Available if p=1? Actually if p=1, there is at least one missing edge; if p=0, there may be missing edges but total even, filling available too. Wait p=0 does not mean no missing edges; m_total could be 2. Filling moves are always available if m_total>0, regardless of p. Our abstraction p only parity; if p=0 and m_total>0, filling is available and toggles p to1. If m_total=0, no filling. We need know whether m_total=0 or just even. The existence of a filling move depends on m_total>0, not just parity. Our abstraction loses that. However if p=1, m_total>0 definitely. If p=0, m_total could be 0 or positive. For P/N, we need know if filling moves exist. In standard impartial games, a heap of size m has SG m mod2; if m=0 no move, if m>0 move. The parity p alone is not enough to know if m=0. But maybe if p=0 and m_total>0, filling moves exist and lead to p=1. We need include a flag f = (m_total > 0)? But m_total can be large; however the SG of a heap of size m is m mod2, and the availability of a move is m>0. For m even >0, the SG is 0, same as m=0, but moves exist. In P-position characterization, we must account for moves. For example, a single component with m=2 has p=0, SG=0 (P), but there is a filling move to m=1 (N). So P-state can have moves. No. The game of adding edges without odd cycles is equivalent to the "bipartite graph game" where the winner is determined by the parity of the number of edges in the graph after applying "Kőnig's theorem"? Let's think of the terminal complete bipartite graph. The total number of moves from initial to terminal is A*B - M. The parity of A*B for N even is A mod2. The final A parity is the parity of the size of one side of a bipartition. This is exactly the parity of the number of vertices in one part. In any bipartite graph, the parity of the size of a part in a connected component is fixed if the component has even order, and free if odd order. The game of adding edges maybe equivalent to players choosing the parity of the final part by playing on the odd-order components. The even-order components with both color classes odd (OO) act as "switches" that flip the parity of an odd component when merged. This is exactly the (k,e) game. The p (missing edges) is like a Nim heap that can be used as a pass. The full game might be the disjunctive sum of the (k,e) game and a Nim heap of size m_total? This condition seems plausible! Let's express in terms of original variables:
For N even:
- k = # odd-order components.
- e = # even-order components with both color classes odd.
- p = m_total mod2.
Then Takahashi wins iff:
  - k = 0 and p = 0, OR
  - k >= 4 and (e + p) is even.
Aoki wins iff k = 2, or k=0,p=1, or k>=4 and e+p odd.

Can we simplify? Since k is even. Let's define q = (e + p) mod2. For k>=4, P iff q=0. For k=0, P iff p=0. For k=2, N.

What about k=0 and q? q = e+p. If p=0, q=e. P regardless of e. So k=0 overrides q.

Now, can we compute k,e,p easily?
- k: number of connected components with odd number of vertices.
- e: number of connected components with even number of vertices and both color classes odd.
- p: parity of total missing edges inside components = Σ (a_i*b_i - edge_count_i) mod2.

Now, can we prove this condition? Let's attempt.

For N even, k even. The game outcome seems:
- k=2: N.
- k=0: P iff p=0.
- k>=4: P iff e+p even.

We can prove by induction using move effects on (k,e,p) and the fact that z (EE components) doesn't affect outcome? Need ensure moves involving z don't break. Let's analyze move effects on the proposed P condition.

Let P_condition:
C(k,e,p):
if k==0: p==0
elif k==2: False (N)
else (k>=4): (e+p)%2==0

We need show from C, all moves lead to not C; from not C, some move to C.

We'll need consider all move types including z. But maybe z changes under moves; however C doesn't depend on z. We need show regardless of z, the move effects on (k,e,p) are as we listed, and z changes don't affect C. The legality of moves may depend on z, but if a move is legal, its effect on (k,e,p) is one of the listed. We can verify each move type:

Let's list move effects on (k,e,p) (z changes omitted):
Filling: p -> 1-p.

Connecting:
A. Odd+Odd:
  - c odd: k -> k-2, e -> e+1, p -> p.
  - c even: k -> k-2, e -> e, p -> 1-p.
B. Odd+OO:
  - c odd: k -> k, e -> e-1, p -> p.
C. Odd+EE:
  - c even: k -> k, e -> e, p -> 1-p.
D. OO+OO:
  - c even: k -> k, e -> e-2, p -> 1-p.
E. OO+EE:
  - c even: k -> k, e -> e, p -> 1-p.
F. EE+EE:
  - c even: k -> k, e -> e, p -> 1-p.

Note: Odd+OO c odd requires e>=1; OO+OO requires e>=2; Odd+Odd requires k>=2; Odd+EE requires z>=1; etc.

Now prove C is a P-position.

Case k=0, p=0:
- Filling: if exists, p=1 -> not C (k=0,p=1).
- Connecting moves:
  * OO+OO: k=0, e-=2, p=1 -> not C (p=1).
  * OO+EE: e unchanged, p=1 -> not C.
  * EE+EE: p=1 -> not C.
  * Odd moves impossible. So all moves to not C. Good.

Case k>=4, e+p even:
Need show all moves lead to not C (i.e., either k=2, or k=0 with p=1, or k>=4 with e+p odd).

- Filling: p toggles -> e+p odd -> not C (k>=4).
- Odd+Odd:
  * c odd: k' = k-2, e' = e+1, p' = p. e'+p' = e+1+p = (e+p)+1 odd. If k'=2 (when k=4), then k'=2 -> not C. If k'=0 impossible (k>=4 -> k'>=2). If k'>=4, e+p odd -> not C. Good.
  * c even: k' = k-2, e' = e, p' = 1-p. e'+p' = e+1-p = (e+p)+1 - 2p? Mod2, e+1-p ≡ e+p+1 (since -p ≡ p mod2). So odd. If k'=2 (k=4) -> not C; if k'>=4 -> not C. Good.
- Odd+OO:
  k' = k, e' = e-1, p' = p. e'+p' = e-1+p = (e+p)+1 odd. k>=4 -> not C. Good.
- Odd+EE:
  k'=k, e'=e, p'=1-p. e'+p' = e+1-p ≡ e+p+1 odd -> not C.
- OO+OO:
  k'=k, e'=e-2, p'=1-p. e'+p' = e-2+1-p ≡ e+p+1 odd -> not C.
- OO+EE:
  e'=e, p'=1-p -> e+p+1 odd -> not C.
- EE+EE:
  same -> not C.
Thus all moves from C go to not C.

Case k=2: C false (N). Need show there is a move to C.
State k=2, any e,p. There are two odd components.
- If p=0: choose odd-odd merge with c odd. Then k'=0, e'=e+1, p'=0. C for k=0 requires p=0 -> true. So move to C.
- If p=1: choose odd-odd merge with c even. Then k'=0, e'=e, p'=0. C true. Is c-even orientation legal? Need ensure. For two odd components, c-even may be 0 if both are isolated. But if p=1, there is at least one missing edge somewhere, so not both odd components are isolated? Wait k=2 could be two isolated plus some even component with m=1? Let's try to derive the game outcome using the "strategy stealing" on final A parity. For N even, target r = 1 XOR M mod2. The game is equivalent to players choosing a final bipartition. The final A parity is determined by the orientations of odd-order components. The even-order components with both color classes odd (OO) contribute 1 to A parity regardless of orientation; EE contribute 0. Odd-order components each contribute a free bit. So final A parity = (number of OO components mod2) XOR (XOR of free bits of odd components). Let e_mod = e mod2. The free bits are the orientations of the k odd components. Initially, each odd component has a free bit. A connecting move between two odd components with c odd consumes two free bits and creates an OO component (contributing 1) or with c even creates an EE component (contributing 0). The players choose which. A connecting move between an odd component and an OO component flips the odd component's free bit? Actually it consumes the OO component (contributing e changes) and the odd component remains free but its internal type flips; the free bit remains. So the number of free bits k is unchanged, but e decreases by1 and the odd component's type flips. Does flipping type affect the free bit? The free bit is the orientation of the odd component; flipping type changes which color class is odd, but the free bit still can be 0/1. The final contribution of that odd component is its free bit. So the set of free bits is unchanged, just relabeled. Thus odd+OO move simply removes one fixed contribution (e--), leaving k free bits. Odd+EE move removes an EE (no e change) and leaves k free bits, but p toggles? Wait odd+EE is c even, so it creates no new missing edges? It toggles p. In terms of final parity, it doesn't change k or e, but consumes a move. It's like a pass.

The filling moves are also passes that don't change k,e.

Thus the strategic game is about reducing k (by pairing odd components) and e (by absorbing odd components), with passes (filling and c-even moves) that toggle p. The final A parity target is e_mod XOR (XOR of free bits). The players can choose free bits when pairing odd components? Actually when pairing two odd components, the resulting even component's contribution (OO=1 or EE=0) is determined by c parity: c odd -> OO (1), c even -> EE (0). This choice also determines the XOR of the two free bits? Let's see: Two odd components have free bits x,y. The final contribution of the merged even component is x XOR y? Since the merged component's A parity is the sum of the chosen contributions of the two components. If we choose orientation, we fix x XOR y to either 0 or1. The resulting even component contribution is exactly x XOR y. If it's OO, contribution 1; if EE, contribution 0. So the player making the odd-odd merge chooses the XOR of the two free bits (and thereby the contribution). Thus the game is: there are k free bits initially; a move pairs two free bits and replaces them by a fixed bit (0 or1) chosen by the player. Odd+OO move removes a fixed bit (e) without changing free bits. Passes toggle p.

This is exactly a game of pairing free bits. The final A parity is the XOR of all fixed bits (from OO components and from pairings). The players alternate moves; passes can change turn. The outcome depends on whether the first player can force the final XOR to target.

This is similar to the game "Turning Turtles" or "Nim" where free bits are tokens; pairing two tokens lets the current player set their XOR. If there are k free bits, the game of pairing them has a known outcome: with k=0, no choices; k=2, the player who pairs them sets the final bit (since no free bits left) and can choose target; but the opponent may have passes to control who makes the pairing. With k>=4, more complex.

Define:
- k = number of free bits (odd components).
- e = number of fixed 1 bits (OO components).
- p = parity of number of pass moves available? Pass moves are filling missing edges and c-even connecting moves. But c-even connecting moves also change k/e? Let's see:
  * Filling: pass, p toggles.
  * Odd+EE: pass, p toggles, k/e unchanged.
  * OO+EE: pass, p toggles, e unchanged.
  * EE+EE: pass, p toggles.
  * OO+OO: not a pass; it removes two fixed 1 bits and adds a fixed 0 bit (EE), so e decreases by2, p toggles. This changes the target base.
  * Odd+OO: removes one fixed 1 bit, k unchanged, p unchanged (c odd). This is not a pass; it changes e.
  * Odd+Odd: pairs two free bits, k-=2, e += (1 if c odd else 0), p unchanged (c odd) or toggles (c even). Wait c even also toggles p and adds EE (e unchanged). So odd-odd c even is a pairing that also toggles p.

- Build adjacency list, visited.
- For each component, count vertices n, edges e_cnt (sum of degrees /2), and bipartition sizes a,b via DFS/BFS.
- Since graph is bipartite, color with 0/1.
- For each component:
  - if n % 2 == 1: k += 1; if n == 1: odd_isolated_count += 1.
  - if n % 2 == 0 and a % 2 == 1 and b % 2 == 1: e += 1.
  - m = a*b - e_cnt; p ^= (m & 1).
- M total edges.

=== ANSWER ===
```python
import sys
sys.setrecursionlimit(1 << 25)

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.readline
    N, M = map(int, input().split())
    g = [[] for _ in range(N)]
    for _ in range(M):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        u -= 1
        v -= 1
        g[u].append(v)
        g[v].append(u)

    visited = [False] * N
    color = [-1] * N

    odd_components = 0          # k
    oo_even_components = 0      # e
    missing_parity = 0          # p
    isolated_odd_components = 0

    for s in range(N):
        if visited[s]:
            continue

        stack = [s]
        visited[s] = True
        color[s] = 0
        cnt = [0, 0]
        edge_sum = 0

        while stack:
            v = stack.pop()
            cnt[color[v]] += 1
            edge_sum += len(g[v])
            for to in g[v]:
                if not visited[to]:
                    visited[to] = True
                    color[to] = color[v] ^ 1
                    stack.append(to)

        edge_sum //= 2
        n = cnt[0] + cnt[1]
        a, b = cnt

        if n % 2 == 1:
            odd_components += 1
            if n == 1:
                isolated_odd_components += 1
        else:
            if a % 2 == 1 and b % 2 == 1:
                oo_even_components += 1

        missing_parity ^= (a * b - edge_sum) & 1

    if N % 2 == 1:
        if M % 2 == 1:
            print("Aoki")
        else:
            print("Takahashi")
    else:
        k = odd_components
        e = oo_even_components
        p = missing_parity

        if k == 0:
            aoki = (p == 1)
        elif k == 2:
            if isolated_odd_components == 2:
                aoki = (M % 2 == e % 2)
            else:
                aoki = True
        else:
            aoki = ((e + p) % 2 == 1)

        print("Aoki" if aoki else "Takahashi")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
